  1. Source slide page 1 (extracted text): ENGINEERING MATHEMATICS - I Unit - 3: Partial Differential Equations Department of Science and Humanities EV0000174 · EV0000175 · EV0000176
  2. Source slide page 2 (extracted text): Contents 1 Formation of PDE by elimination of arbitrary functions 2 Solutions of Partial Differential Equations 3 Solution of First-Order Equations 2 / 11 EV0000177 · EV0000178 · EV0000179
  3. Source slide page 3 (extracted text): Formation of PDE by elimination of arbitrary functions 1 Partial differential equations can be obtained by eliminating arbitrary functions from a given equation 2 A first-order partial differential equation can be derived from an equation involving a single arbitrary function, while a second- order equation may arise from an expression containing two arbi- trary functions 3 However, it is not always guaranteed that an nth-order partial differ- ential equation can be formed from an equation involving n arbitrary functions 4 In some cases, higher-order partial derivatives may be required to eliminate all n arbitrary functions, leading to a partial differential equation of order higher than n. Moreover, such a resulting higher- order equation may not be unique 3 / 11 EV0000180 · EV0000181 · EV0000182
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): Example: Formation of a PDE by eliminating an arbitrary function Eliminate the arbitrary function from z = f(x + y) to obtain a first-order partial differential equation. Answer: Let u = x + y. Then z = f(u). Differentiating with respect to x: ∂z ∂u = f′(u) · = f′(x + y) · 1 = f′(x + y) ∂x ∂x Differentiating with respect to y: ∂z ∂u = f′(u) · = f′(x + y) · 1 = f′(x + y) ∂y ∂y From above, ∂z ∂z = ∂x ∂y So, the required PDE is: ∂z −∂z = 0 ∂x ∂y 4 / 11 EV0000183 · EV0000184 · EV0000185
  5. Source slide page 5 (extracted text): Example: 2 Obtain a second-order partial differential equation by eliminating the arbitrary functions from u = f(x + ct) + g(x −ct). Solution Differentiating the given equation partially with respect to x and t, we obtain ∂u ∂u = f′(x + ct) + g′(x −ct), = cf′(x + ct) −cg′(x −ct), ∂x ∂t ∂2u ∂2u = f′′(x + ct) + g′′(x −ct), = c2f′′(x + ct) + c2g′′(x −ct). ∂x2 ∂t2 Hence, we obtain the equation ∂2u ∂2u = c2 ∂t2 ∂x2 5 / 11 EV0000186 · EV0000187 · EV0000188
  6. Source slide page 6 (extracted text): Example: 3 Given z = f(x2 + y), where f is an arbitrary function, form the partial differential equation by eliminating the arbitrary function Solution: 1 Differentiate with respect to x: ∂z = f′(x2 + y) · 2x ∂x 2 Differentiate with respect to y: ∂z = f′(x2 + y) · 1 = f′(x2 + y) ∂y 3 Eliminate f′ by dividing the first equation by the second: ∂z 2xf′(x2 + y) ∂x = = 2x ∂z f′(x2 + y) ∂y Required PDE: ∂z −2x∂z = 0 ∂x ∂y 6 / 11 EV0000189 · EV0000190 · EV0000191
  7. Source slide page 7 (extracted text): Example: 4 Given z = f(x2 + y2) + x, where f is an arbitrary function, form the partial differential equation by eliminating the arbitrary function Solution: 1 Differentiate with respect to x: ∂z = f′(x2 + y2) · 2x + 1 ∂x 2 Differentiate with respect to y: ∂z = f′(x2 + y2) · 2y ∂y 3 Eliminate f′ by solving the second equation for f′(x2 + y2): 1 ∂z f′(x2 + y2) = (for y̸ = 0) 2y ∂y Substitute into the first equation: ∂z = 2x 1 ∂z + 1 = x ∂z + 1 ∂x 2y ∂y y ∂y Required PDE: ∂z −x ∂z = 1 ∂x y ∂y 7 / 11 EV0000192 · EV0000193 · EV0000194
  8. Source slide page 8 (extracted text): Solutions of Partial Differential Equations A solution of a partial differential equation is a relation between the dependent and independent variables that satisfies the partial differential equation It is known that a partial differential equation is formed by eliminating arbitrary constants and arbitrary functions. These relations are solutions of the partial differential equation formed There are two types of solutions for partial differential equations: 1. Solution containing arbitrary constants 2. Solution containing arbitrary functions 8 / 11 EV0000195 · EV0000196 · EV0000197
  9. Source slide page 9 (extracted text): Complete Solution & General Solution A solution of a partial differential equation which contains as many arbitrary constants as the number of independent variables is called the complete solution A solution of a partial differential equation, which contains as many arbitrary functions as the order of the equation, is called the general solution 9 / 11 EV0000198 · EV0000199 · EV0000200
  10. Source slide page 10 (extracted text): Example Suppose we have the relation: z = f(x + y) where f is an arbitrary (differentiable) function. Now, ∂z = f′(x + y); ∂z = f′(x + y). Therefore, ∂x ∂y ∂z ∂z = ∂x ∂y The partial differential equation: ∂z −∂z = 0 ∂x ∂y Conclusion: The equation ∂z −∂z = 0 is a partial differential equation formed by ∂x ∂y eliminating the arbitrary function f from the original relation. The orig- inal relation z = f(x+y) is a solution to this partial differential equation 10 / 11 EV0000201 · EV0000202 · EV0000203
  11. Source slide page 11 (extracted text): Solution of First-Order Equations An equation containing x, y, z, p, q defines a first order partial differential equation, that is F(x, y, z, p, q) = 0 (1) General solution: A relation of the form ϕ(u, v) = 0, where ϕ is an arbitrary function of u = u(x, y, z), v = v(x, y, z) and satisfies Eq. (1) is called a general solution Particular solution: The solution obtained by determining the arbitrary function in the general solution by using some specified condition is called a particular solution 11 / 11 EV0000204 · EV0000205 · EV0000206

  3. Source slide page 3 (extracted text): Definition: Differential Equation A Differential Equation is an equation that involves: an independent variable, a dependent variable, and derivatives of the dependent variable with respect to the independent variable. Example (ODE): dy + y = ex dx x: independent variable y: dependent variable dx:dy derivative of y with respect to x 3 / 10 EV0000213 · EV0000214 · EV0000215
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): Classification of Differential Equations Differential Equations Ordinary Differential Partial Differential Equations (ODEs) Equations (PDEs) 4 / 10 EV0000216 · EV0000217 · EV0000218
  5. Source slide page 5 (extracted text): ODEs vs PDEs Ordinary Differential Equations (ODEs) Having Derivatives with respect to only one independent variable The unknown function depends on a single variable Example: dy + y = ex dx Partial Differential Equations (PDEs) Involves partial derivatives with respect to two or more inde- pendent variables The unknown function depends on multiple variables Example: ∂u = α∂2u ∂t ∂x2 5 / 10 EV0000219 · EV0000220 · EV0000221
  6. Source slide page 6 (extracted text): Partial Differential Equations A partial differential equation (PDE) is an equation that involves partial derivatives of a function with several independent variables In contrast to ordinary differential equations (ODEs), which have one independent variable, PDEs involve two or more Examples: One-dimensional heat conduction equation: ut = uxx Laplace equation: uxx + uyy = 0 One-dimensional wave equation: utt = uxx 6 / 10 EV0000222 · EV0000223 · EV0000224
  7. Source slide page 7 (extracted text): Why Use PDEs? Many physical and social problems have more than two independent variables. Because of this, partial differential equations (PDEs) are the best tool to study these problems. PDEs help us un- derstand many real-world phenomena, including: Waves, like those in vibrating strings Heat flow Fluid movement Electromagnetic fields 7 / 10 EV0000225 · EV0000226 · EV0000227
  8. Source slide page 8 (extracted text): Order and Degree of Partial Differential Equations Order: The order of a partial differential equation is the order of the highest order partial derivative occurring in the equation Degree: The degree of a partial differential equation is the degree of the highest order partial derivative occurring in the equation af- ter the equation has been made free of radicals and fractions with respect to the partial derivatives Example: x ∂z + y ∂z = z (Order = 1, Degree = 1) ∂x ∂y a2 ∂2z + ∂2z = ∂2z (Order = 2, Degree = 1) ∂x2 ∂y2 ∂t2 ∂z 3 ∂2z + = cos(x + y) (Order = 2, Degree = 1) ∂x ∂y2 ∂2z ∂2z + = 0 (Order = 2, Degree = 1) ∂x2 ∂y2 8 / 10 EV0000228 · EV0000229 · EV0000230
  9. Source slide page 9 (extracted text): Standard Notations Let z be a dependent variable and x, y be independent variables, that is, z = z(x, y). Then, p = ∂z/∂x = zx q = ∂z/∂y = zy r = ∂2z/∂x2 = zxx s = ∂2z/∂x∂y = zxy t = ∂2z/∂y2 = zyy 9 / 10 EV0000231 · EV0000232 · EV0000233
  10. Source slide page 10 (extracted text): First and Second order PDE’s An equation containing x, y, z, p, q defines a first order partial differential equation, that is f(x, y, z, p, q) = 0 (1) This equation is linear, if it is linear in p, q An equation containing x, y, z, p, q, r, s, t defines a second order partial differential equation, that is g(x, y, z, p, q, r, s, t) = 0 (2) This equation is linear, if it is linear in p, q, r, s, t 10 / 10 EV0000234 · EV0000235 · EV0000236

8Method of separation of variables

This lesson is indexed from the source video titled Method of separation of variables. Its complete timestamped transcript is preserved below in segment order. The original video path and content hash are recorded in datasets/lecture-corpus.json. Transcript text is source wording; it has not been silently corrected or rewritten. A candidate slide deck is grouped here according to the current matching score; the exact path and page locators are listed in the source evidence. EV0000074 · EV0000075

Graphs and diagrams

Important points and why

  1. Source slide page 1 (extracted text): ENGINEERING MATHEMATICS - I Unit - 3: Partial Differential Equations Department of Science and Humanities EV0000342 · EV0000343 · EV0000344
  2. Source slide page 2 (extracted text): Contents 1 Method of Separation of Variables 2 / 7 EV0000345 · EV0000346 · EV0000347
  3. Source slide page 3 (extracted text): Method of Separation of Variables It is based on the idea that a solution can be written as a product of functions, each depending on only one independent variable Separation of variables provides closed-form solutions for many fun- damental partial differential equations—the one-dimensional heat equation, the wave equation, and Laplace’s equation—by reducing each PDE to a set of ordinary differential equations 3 / 7 EV0000348 · EV0000349 · EV0000350
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): Working Rule 1 For a P.D.E. in the function u of two independent variables x and y, assume that the required solution is separable, i.e., u(x, y) = X(x)Y (y), (1) where X(x) is a function of x alone and Y (y) is a function of y alone 2 Substitution of u from (1) and its derivatives reduces the P.D.E. to the form f(X, X′, X′′, . . .) = g(Y, Y ′, Y ′′, . . .) (2) which is separable in X and Y 3 Since the L.H.S. of (2) is a function of x alone and the R.H.S. of (2) is a function of y alone, then (2) must be equal to a common constant, say k 4 / 7 EV0000351 · EV0000352 · EV0000353
  5. Source slide page 5 (extracted text): Working Rule (contd.) 1 Thus (2) reduces to f(X, X′, X′′, . . .) = k (3) g(Y, Y ′, Y ′′, . . .) = k (4) 2 The determination of a solution to P.D.E. reduces to the determi- nation of solutions to two O.D.E.s (with appropriate conditions). 5 / 7 EV0000354 · EV0000355 · EV0000356
  6. Source slide page 6 (extracted text): Example Use the separation of variables technique to solve 3ux + 2uy = 0 with u(x, 0) = 4e−x. Solution: Assume u(x, y) = X(x)Y (y). Then the P.D.E. becomes 3X′Y + 2XY ′ = 0 or X′ = −2 Y ′ = k (constant) X 3 Y Solving X′ −kX = 0, we get X(x) = c1ekx. 2 ky. Similarly, Y ′ + 32kY = 0, so Y (y) = c2e−3 2 ky = cekxe−32 ky = cek(x−32 y).So u(x, y) = X(x)Y (y) = c1ekx · c2e−3 Given that 4e−x = u(x, 0) = X(x)Y (0) = cekx, Thus c = 4, k = −1. Hence, the required solution is u(x, y) = 4e−12 (2x−3y) 6 / 7 EV0000357 · EV0000358 · EV0000359
  7. Source slide page 7 (extracted text): Example Use separation of variables to solve 5 ux −4 uy = 0, u(x, 0) = 3e2x. Solution. Assume u(x, y) = X(x) Y (y). Then, ux = X′(x)Y (y), uy = X(x)Y ′(y), so 5X′Y −4X Y ′ = 0 X′ 4 Y ′=⇒ = = k. X 5 Y Solve ODEs. X′/X = k ⇒X(x) = A ekx, Y ′ 5 5 k = ⇒Y (y) = B e 4 y. Y 4k Combine. 5 5 u(x, y) = AB ekx e 4 k y = C ek (x+ 4 y). Initial condition. u(x, 0) = C ekx = 3e2x =⇒C = 3, k = 2. 5 Final answer. u(x, y) = 3 e2x+ 2 y. 7 / 7 EV0000360 · EV0000361 · EV0000362

  3. Source slide page 3 (extracted text): Modelling of the one-dimensional heat equation Consider a long, thin bar (or rod) of constant cross-sectional area A and homogeneous conducting material. Let ρ be the density of the material, c be the specific heat, and k be the thermal conductivity of the material. We assume that the surface of the bar is insulated so that the heat flow is along parallel lines which are perpendicular to the area A. 3 / 7 EV0000390 · EV0000391 · EV0000392
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): Choose one end of the bar as origin and the direction of heat flow as the positive x-axis. Let u(x, t) be the temperature at a distance x from 0. If ∆u be the temperature change in the slab of thickness ∆x of the bar, and time change ∆t, then the quantity of heat in this slab is (specific heat) × (mass of the element slab) × (change in temperature) = c A ρ ∆x ∆u. Hence, the rate of change (i.e., increase) of heat in the slab at time t is ∆u ∂u c Aρ ∆x lim = c Aρ ∆x . ∆t→0 ∆t ∂t 4 / 7 EV0000393 · EV0000394 · EV0000395
  5. Source slide page 5 (extracted text): Let R1 be the rate of inflow of heat at x in the slab and R2 be the rate of outflow of heat at x + ∆x. Then ∂u c Aρ ∆x = R1 −R2, (1) ∂t where by Fourier’s law ∂u ∂u R1 = −kA and R2 = −kA . ∂x x ∂x x+∆x The negative sign reflects the fact that heat flows from regions of higher temperature to regions of lower temperature. 5 / 7 EV0000396 · EV0000397 · EV0000398
  6. Source slide page 6 (extracted text): ∂u Since is negative and R1, R2 are positive, the rate of increase of ∂t heat at time t is h ∂u ∂u i R1 −R2 = kA ∂x x+∆x − ∂x x . (2) Combining (1) and (2), we get ∂u h ∂u ∂u i c Aρ ∆x = kA − . ∂t ∂x x+∆x ∂x x Hence ∂u − ∂u ∂u k ∂x x+∆x ∂x x = . ∂t cρ ∆x As ∆x →0, the difference quotient becomes a second derivative, ∂u k ∂2u = , ∂t cρ ∂x2 k where is the thermal diffusivity of the material. cρ 6 / 7 EV0000399 · EV0000400 · EV0000401
  7. Source slide page 7 (extracted text): Setting k α2 = cρ, we obtain the one-dimensional heat equation ∂u ∂2u = α2 . ∂t ∂x2 7 / 7 EV0000402 · EV0000403 · EV0000404

10Solution of one dimensional heat equation

Graphs and diagrams

Important points and why

  1. Source slide page 1 (extracted text): ENGINEERING MATHEMATICS - I Unit - 3: Partial Differential Equations Department of Science and Humanities EV0000405 · EV0000406 · EV0000407
  2. Source slide page 2 (extracted text): Contents 1 Applications of PDE – Solution of the heat equation by the method of separation of variables 2 / 6 EV0000408 · EV0000409 · EV0000410
  3. Source slide page 3 (extracted text): Solution of the heat equation The heat equation is ∂u ∂2u = c2 (1) ∂t ∂x2 Let u = XT (2) where X is a function of x only and T is a function of t only, be a solution of (1). Then ∂u ′ ∂2u = XT and = X′′T ∂t ∂x2 Substituting in (1), we have XT ′ = c2X′′T Separating the variables, we get X′′ 1 T ′ = (3) X c2 T 3 / 6 EV0000411 · EV0000412 · EV0000413
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): Solution of the heat equation (contd.) Now the L.H.S. of (3) is a function of x only and the R.H.S. is a function of t only. Since x and t are independent variables, this equation can hold only when both sides reduce to a constant, say k. The equation (3) leads to the ordinary differential equations d2X dT −kX = 0 and −kc2T = 0 (4) dx2 dt 4 / 6 EV0000414 · EV0000415 · EV0000416
  5. Source slide page 5 (extracted text): Solution of the heat equation (contd.) Solving equations (4), we get (i) When k is positive and = p2, say X = c1epx + c2e−px, T = c3ec2p2t u = (c1epx + c2e−px) · c3ec2p2t (5) (ii) When k is negative and = −p2, say X = c1 cos px + c2 sin px, T = c3e−c2p2t u = (c1 cos px + c2 sin px) · c3e−c2p2t (6) (iii) When k = 0 X = c1x + c2, T = c3 u = (c1x + c2)c3 (7) 5 / 6 EV0000417 · EV0000418 · EV0000419
  6. Source slide page 6 (extracted text): Solution of the heat equation (contd.) Thus the various possible solutions of the heat equation (1) are: u = (c1epx + c2e−px) · c3ec2p2t (5) u = (c1 cos px + c2 sin px) · c3e−c2p2t (6) u = (c1x + c2)c3 (7) Of these three solutions, we have to choose that solution which is consis- tent with the physical nature of the problem. Since u decreases as time t increases, the only suitable solution of the heat equation is u = (c1 cos px + c2 sin px)c3e−c2p2t 6 / 6 EV0000420 · EV0000421 · EV0000422

Source slide page 3 (extracted text): Higher-Order Linear Equations with Constant Coefficients We have already learned how to solve higher-order linear ordinary differential equations with constant coefficients. These same meth- ods can also be used to solve higher-order linear partial differential equations with constant coefficients For example, consider the following partial differential equation ∂nz ∂nz ∂nz A0 + A1 n−1 + A2 n−2 + · · · ∂xn ∂x ∂y ∂x ∂y2 (1) ∂nz ∂nz · · · + An−1 n−1 + An = f(x, y) . ∂x ∂y ∂yn where Ai, i = 0, 1, . . . , n are constants. 3 / 16 EV0000429 · EV0000430 · EV0000431
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): Higher-Order Linear Equations with Constant Coefficients (contd.) Denote ∂z ∂z ∂2z ∂2z p = = Dz, q = = D′z, D2z = , DD′z = ∂x ∂y ∂x2 ∂x∂y etc. Then, Eq. (1) can be written as n F(D, D′)z = X ArDn−r(D′)rz = f(x, y). (2) r=0 4 / 16 EV0000432 · EV0000433 · EV0000434
  5. Source slide page 5 (extracted text): Higher-Order Linear Equations with Constant Coefficients (contd.) As in the case of ordinary differential equations, the general solution of Eq. (2) can be written as z = (complementary function) + (particular integral), where the complementary function is the solution of the homogeneous equation F(D, D′)z = 0 and the particular integral is a solution satisfying the non-homogeneous equation (2) and does not contain any arbitrary constants. 5 / 16 EV0000435 · EV0000436 · EV0000437
  6. Source slide page 6 (extracted text): Complementary Function Consider the linear, homogeneous equation F(D, D′)z = 0. (3) If F(D, D′) can be factorised into linear factors of the form (aiD + biD′ + ci), then F(D, D′) is said to be reducible. Otherwise, it is said to be irre- ducible 6 / 16 EV0000438 · EV0000439 · EV0000440
  7. Source slide page 7 (extracted text): Complementary Function (contd.) F(D, D′) may contain some reducible and some irreducible factors. We have the following examples. (D2 −D′2)z = (D −D′)(D + D′)z = 0 (4) (D3−6D2D′+11DD′2−6D′3)z = (D−D′)(D−2D′)(D−3D′)z = 0 (5) (2D2 −D′)z = 0 (6) (D3 + D2D′ + D′3)z = 0 (7) (D3 −D2D′ + DD′ −D′2)z = (D −D′)(D2 + D′2)z = 0 (8) Eqs. (4) and (5) are the reducible forms, Eqs. (6) and (7) are irreducible forms and Eq. (8) has one reducible and one irreducible factor. Note that we will discuss only the equations which are in “re- ducible form”. 7 / 16 EV0000441 · EV0000442 · EV0000443

Source slide page 8 (extracted text): Distinct Roots Consider the solution of the partial differential equation with one linear factor, that is, the solution of (aiD + biD′ + ci)z = 0, or aip + biq = −ciz. (9) The solution of Eq. (9) is given by z = e−(cix)/ai ϕi(bix −aiy) (10) 8 / 16 EV0000444 · EV0000445 · EV0000446
  9. Source slide page 9 (extracted text): Distinct Roots (contd.) Some particular cases: a) If the linear factor is of the form (aiD + biD′), then Eq.(10) simplifies to z = ϕi(bix −aiy) (11) (b) If the linear factor is of the form (biD′ + ci), then we have the differential equation as (biD′ + ci)z = 0 or biqi + ciz = 0. The solutions of the auxiliary equations are bix = k1 and z = k2e−(ciy)/bi Hence, the solution of Eq. (9), when ai = 0, is given by k2 = ϕi(k1), or ze(ciy)/bi = ϕi(bix) or z = e−(ciy)/bi ϕi(bix) (12) 9 / 16 EV0000447 · EV0000448 · EV0000449
  10. Source slide page 10 (extracted text): Distinct Roots (contd.) (c) Similarly, if the linear factor is of the form (aiD + ci), then the solution of Eq.(9), when bi = 0, is given by z = e−cixai ϕi aiy (13) The complementary function is given by z = e−c1xa1 ϕ1 b1x −a1y + e−c2x a2 ϕ2 b2x −a2y + · · · + e−cnx an ϕn bnx −any Similarly, the complementary function in the other cases can be written. 10 / 16 EV0000450 · EV0000451 · EV0000452
  11. Source slide page 11 (extracted text): Distinct Roots (contd.) Note: If all terms in F(D, D′) have the same total degree, then we can set m = D/D′ and look for factors of the equation using m. Example: Find the solutions of the following PDE’s (i) (D2 −D′2)z = 0, (ii) (D3 −6D2D′ + 11DD′2 −6D′3)z = 0, (iii) 3r + 7s + 2t + 7p + 4q + 2z = 0, (iv) 2r −s −t −p + q = 0. (i) We have (D2 −D′2)z = (D + D′)(D −D′)z = 0. For the factor D −D′, we have a1 = 1, b1 = −1. For the factor D + D′, we have a2 = 1, b2 = 1. Therefore, using Eq. (11), we obtain the solution as z = ϕ∗1(−x −y) + ϕ2(x −y) = ϕ1(x + y) + ϕ2(x −y). 11 / 16 EV0000453 · EV0000454 · EV0000455
  12. Source slide page 12 (extracted text): Distinct Roots (contd.) (ii) We have (D3 −6D2D′ + 11DD′2 −6D′3)z ⇒(D −D′)(D −2D′)(D −3D′)z = 0. For the factor D −D′, we have a1 = 1, b1 = −1. For the factor D −2D′, we have a2 = 1, b2 = −2. For the factor D −3D′, we have a3 = 1, b3 = −3. If we set (D/D′) = m, we get the equation as m3 −6m2 + 11m −6 = 0, whose roots are m = 1, 2, 3. Hence, the factors are D −D′, D −2D′, D −3D′. Using Eq. (11), the solution can be written as z = ϕ∗1(−x −y) + ϕ∗2(−2x −y) + ϕ∗3(−3x −y) = ϕ1(x + y) + ϕ2(2x + y) + ϕ3(3x + y). 12 / 16 EV0000456 · EV0000457 · EV0000458
  13. Source slide page 13 (extracted text): Distinct Roots (contd.) The aforementioned note does not apply to the following two problems. (iii) We have (3D2+7DD′+2D′2+7D+4D′+2)z = (3D+D′+1)(D+2D′+2)z = 0. For the factor 3D + D′ + 1, we have a1 = 3, b1 = 1, c1 = 1. For the factor D + 2D′ + 2, we have a2 = 1, b2 = 2, c2 = 2. Using Eq. (10), the solution can be written as z = e−x/3ϕ1(x −3y) + e−2xϕ2(2x −y). 13 / 16 EV0000459 · EV0000460 · EV0000461
  14. Source slide page 14 (extracted text): Distinct Roots (contd.) (iv) We have (2D2 −DD′ −D′2 −D + D′)z = (D −D′)(2D + D′ −1)z = 0. For the factor D −D′, we have a1 = 1, b1 = −1, c1 = 0. For the factor 2D + D′ −1, we have a2 = 2, b2 = 1, c2 = −1. Using Eqs. (10) and (11), the solution can be written as z = ϕ∗1(−x −y) + ex/2ϕ2(x −2y) = ϕ1(x + y) + ex/2ϕ2(x −2y). 14 / 16 EV0000462 · EV0000463 · EV0000464

Source slide page 15 (extracted text): Multiple Factors Let a factor be of multiplicity 2, that is, we have either of the factors (aiD + biD′ + ci)2, or (aiD + ci)2, or (biD′ + ci)2. 1 For the equation (aiD + biD′ + ci)2z = 0, the general solution is given by z = e−(cix)/ai [x ϕi(bix −aiy) + ψi(bix −aiy)] 2 For the equation (aiD + ci)2z = 0, the general solution is given by z = e−(cix)/ai [x ϕi(aiy) + ψi(aiy)] 15 / 16 EV0000465 · EV0000466 · EV0000467
  16. Source slide page 16 (extracted text): Multiple Factors (contd.) 1 For the equation (biD′ + ci)2z = 0, the general solution is z = e−(ciy)/bi [y ϕi(bix) + ψi(bix)] 2 If the factor is of multiplicity m, say (a1D + b1D′ + c1)m, then the general solution is z = e−c1xa1 ψ1(b1x −a1y) + x ψ2(b1x −a1y) + . . . + xm−1 ψm(b1x −a1y) Similarly, we can write the general solution in the other cases. 16 / 16 EV0000468 · EV0000469 · EV0000470

  2. Source slide page 2 (extracted text): Contents 1 Particular Integral when f(x, y) = eax+by 2 Particular Integral when f(x, y) = sin(ax + b) or cos(ax + b) 2 / 11 EV0000474 · EV0000475 · EV0000476
  3. Source slide page 3 (extracted text): Particular Integral when f(x, y) = eax+by Consider the non-homogeneous equation F(D, D′)z = f(x, y) The particular integral is now written as z = [F(D, D′)−1]f(x, y) 3 / 11 EV0000477 · EV0000478 · EV0000479
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): When f(x, y) = eax+by The particular integral in this case is 1 z = [F(D, D′)−1]eax+by = if F(a, b)̸ = 0. F(a, b)eax+by, If F(a, b) = 0, then we write the particular integral as z = ϕ(x, y)eax+by, where ϕ(x, y) = [F(D + a, D′ + b)]−1(1). We expand the operator [F(D+a, D′ +b)]−1 in an infinite series symbol- ically, and determine ϕ(x, y). Then, the particular integral is given by z = ϕ(x, y)eax+by. Note that D−1 and (D′)−1 mean integral with respect to x and y respectively, keeping the other variable as constant 4 / 11 EV0000480 · EV0000481 · EV0000482
  5. Source slide page 5 (extracted text): Problem Find the general solution of the partial differential equation [2D2 −DD′ −(D′)2 + D −D′]z = e2x+3y. Solution: We write F(D, D′) z = 2D2 −D D′ −(D′)2 + D −D′ z = (2D + D′ + 1)(D −D′) z = e2x+3y . The homogeneous equation is (2D + D′ + 1)(D −D′)z = 0 For the factor D −D′, we have a1 = 1, b1 = −1, c1 = 0. For the factor 2D + D′ + 1, we have a2 = 2, b2 = 1, c2 = 1. Therefore, the complementary function as z = e−x/2ϕ1(x −2y) + ϕ2(x + y) 5 / 11 EV0000483 · EV0000484 · EV0000485

Source slide page 3 (extracted text): Problems on finding the solution when RHS=0 Find the solutions of the following partial differential equations (i) (D4 −2D2D′2 + D′4)z = 0, (ii) (4D3 −3DD′2 + D′3)z = 0. Solution (i) We have (D4 −2D2D′2 + D′4)z = (D2 −D′2)2z = (D −D′)2(D + D′)2z = 0. For the factor (D −D′)2, we have a1 = 1, b1 = −1, c1 = 0. For the factor (D + D′)2, we have a2 = 1, b2 = 1, c2 = 0. If we set m = D/D′, we get m4 −2m2 + 1 = 0. The roots are m2 = 1, or m = ±1 which are double roots. Hence, the factors are (D −D′)2 and (D + D′)2. Therefore, the general solution as z = [xϕ∗1(−x −y) + ϕ∗2(−x −y)] + [xψ1(x −y) + ψ2(x −y)] or z = xϕ1(x + y) + ϕ2(x + y) + xψ1(x −y) + ψ2(x −y). 3 / 7 EV0000510 · EV0000511 · EV0000512
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): Problems (contd.) (ii) We have (4D3 −3DD′2 + D′3)z = (D + D′)(4D2 −4DD′ + D′2)z = (D + D′)(2D −D′)2z = 0. For the factor D + D′, we have a1 = 1, b1 = 1, c1 = 0. For the factor (2D −D′)2, we have a2 = 2, b2 = −1, c2 = 0. The general solution as z = ϕ1(x −y) + xψ∗1(−x −2y) + ψ∗2(−x −2y) or z = ϕ1(x −y) + xψ1(x + 2y) + ψ2(x + 2y). 4 / 7 EV0000513 · EV0000514 · EV0000515

Then the auxiliary equation becomes: D′ m2 −m −6 = 0. Solving: 1 ± √1 + 24 1 ± 5 m = = ⇒m = 3, −2. 2 2 For m = 3: a1 = 1, b1 = −3 ⇒characteristic variable −3x −y, For m = −2: a2 = 1, b2 = 2 ⇒characteristic variable 2x −y.   6. Source slide page 6 (extracted text): Problems (contd.) 4. Find the solutions of the following partial differential equations (i) (D2 −2DD′ + D′2)2z = 0, (ii) (D3 −3D2D′ + 3DD′2 −D′3)z = 0. Solution: (i) We observe that (D2 −2DD′ + D′2)2z = (D −D′)4z = 0. So the repeated factor is (D −D′), four times. This corresponds to: a = 1, b = −1, (repeated root).   3. Source slide page 3 (extracted text): Lagrange’s Linear Equation 1 The linear first-order partial differential equation of the form P(x, y, z) p + Q(x, y, z) q = R(x, y, z) (1) is called the Lagrange’s equation in two independent variables x, y 2 Theorem: The general solution of the equation Pp + Qq = R is given by ϕ(u, v) = 0, where ϕ is an arbitrary function and u(x, y, z) = c1, v(x, y, z) = c2 are two linearly independent solutions of the equa- tions dx dy dz = = (2) P Q R (These equations are called the auxiliary or subsidiary equations.) 3 / 9 EV0000267 · EV0000268 · EV0000269
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): Working Rule to Solve Lagrange’s Linear Equation To solve the equation Pp + Qq = R 1 Form the auxiliary equations dx dy dz = = P Q R 2 Solve the auxiliary equations by the method of grouping or the method of multipliers or both to get two independent solutions u = a and v = b, where a, b are arbitrary constants 3 ϕ(u, v) = 0 is the general solution of the equation Pp + Qq = R 4 / 9 EV0000270 · EV0000271 · EV0000272
  5. Source slide page 5 (extracted text): Method of Grouping in Lagrange’s Equation 1 The method of grouping involves pairing two of these ratios at a time to form ordinary differential equations which can be integrated to find the solution 2 Group two ratios at a time: dx dy = P Q dy dz = Q R dx dz = P R 3 Integrate each group to obtain two independent solutions 4 The general solution is given by ϕ(u, v) = 0, where u and v are the two independent solutions obtained 5 / 9 EV0000273 · EV0000274 · EV0000275
  6. Source slide page 6 (extracted text): Example: Suppose the auxiliary equations are: dx dy dz = = x y z Grouping dx = dy : x y Z dx Z dy = =⇒ln x = ln y + c1 =⇒x = c2 x y y Grouping dx = dz : x z Z dx Z dz = =⇒ln x = ln z + c3 =⇒x = c4 x z z The general solution is: x x F , = 0 y z 6 / 9 EV0000276 · EV0000277 · EV0000278
  7. Source slide page 7 (extracted text): Method of Multipliers in Lagrange’s Equation 1 Let the auxiliary equations be dx = dy = dz p Q R 2 Let l, m, n be constants or functions of x, y, z. Then by the property of ratio and proportion, we have dx dy dz ldx + mdy + ndz Each Ratio = = = = p Q R lp + mQ + nR 3 l, m, n are chosen in such a way that lP + mQ + nR = 0. Thus ldx + mdy + ndz = 0 4 Solve this differential equation, and the solution is u = c1 5 Similarly, choose another set of multipliers (l1, m1, n1) and the second solution is v = C2 6 Required general solution is ϕ(u, v) = 0, where u and v are the two independent solutions 7 / 9 EV0000279 · EV0000280 · EV0000281
  8. Source slide page 8 (extracted text): Example: Suppose the auxiliary equations are: dx dy dz = = y −z z −x x −y Choose multipliers l = 1, m = 1, n = 1. Then, dx + dy + dz dx + dy + dz Each Ratio = = y −z + z −x + x −y 0 This gives dx + dy + dz = 0. On integration, it gives x + y + z = C1. 8 / 9 EV0000282 · EV0000283 · EV0000284
  9. Source slide page 9 (extracted text): Differential Concept in Lagrange’s Equation 1 Given the auxiliary equations: dx = dy = dz P Q R 2 Sometimes, instead of grouping or using multipliers directly, we use the differential concept to form a linear combination of differentials that is easier to integrate 3 It is essentially the same as the method of multipliers, but empha- sizes the use of differentials and their integration 4 Multipliers may be chosen (more than once) such that the numerator ldx + mdy + ndz is an exact differential of the denominator lP + mQ + nR 5 Finally, ldx+mdy+ndz is combined with a fraction of dx = dy = dz to lP+mQ+nR P Q R get an integral 9 / 9 EV0000285 · EV0000286 · EV0000287

Important points and why

  1. Source slide page 1 (extracted text): ENGINEERING MATHEMATICS - I Unit - 3: Partial Differential Equations Department of Science and Humanities EV0000363 · EV0000364 · EV0000365
  2. Source slide page 2 (extracted text): Contents 1 Method of Separation of Variables 2 / 7 EV0000366 · EV0000367 · EV0000368
  3. Source slide page 3 (extracted text): Method of Separation of Variables The method of separation of variables is a powerful and widely used technique to solve certain types of Partial Differential Equations (PDEs), especially when the PDE and boundary conditions are defined over a rectangular or regular domain It is based on the idea that a solution can be written as a product of functions, each depending on only one independent variable Separation of variables provides closed-form solutions for many fun- damental partial differential equations—the one-dimensional heat equation, the wave equation, and Laplace’s equation—by reducing each PDE to a set of ordinary differential equations 3 / 7 EV0000369 · EV0000370 · EV0000371
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): Working Rule 1 For a P.D.E. in the function u of two independent variables x and y, assume that the required solution is separable, i.e., u(x, y) = X(x)Y (y), (1) where X(x) is a function of x alone and Y (y) is a function of y alone 2 Substitution of u from (1) and its derivatives reduces the P.D.E. to the form f(X, X′, X′′, . . .) = g(Y, Y ′, Y ′′, . . .) (2) which is separable in X and Y 3 Since the L.H.S. of (2) is a function of x alone and the R.H.S. of (2) is a function of y alone, then (2) must be equal to a common constant, say k 4 / 7 EV0000372 · EV0000373 · EV0000374
  5. Source slide page 5 (extracted text): Working Rule (contd.) 1 Thus (2) reduces to f(X, X′, X′′, . . .) = k (3) g(Y, Y ′, Y ′′, . . .) = k (4) 2 The determination of a solution to P.D.E. reduces to the determi- nation of solutions to two O.D.E.s (with appropriate conditions). 5 / 7 EV0000375 · EV0000376 · EV0000377
  6. Source slide page 6 (extracted text): Example Use the separation of variables technique to solve 3ux + 2uy = 0 with u(x, 0) = 4e−x. Solution: Assume u(x, y) = X(x)Y (y). Then the P.D.E. becomes 3X′Y + 2XY ′ = 0 or X′ = −2 Y ′ = k (constant) X 3 Y Solving X′ −kX = 0, we get X(x) = c1ekx. 2 ky. Similarly, Y ′ + 32kY = 0, so Y (y) = c2e−3 2 ky = cekxe−32 ky = cek(x−32 y).So u(x, y) = X(x)Y (y) = c1ekx · c2e−3 Given that 4e−x = u(x, 0) = X(x)Y (0) = cekx, Thus c = 4, k = −1. Hence, the required solution is u(x, y) = 4e−12 (2x−3y) 6 / 7 EV0000378 · EV0000379 · EV0000380
  7. Source slide page 7 (extracted text): Example Use separation of variables to solve 5 ux −4 uy = 0, u(x, 0) = 3e2x. Solution. Assume u(x, y) = X(x) Y (y). Then, ux = X′(x)Y (y), uy = X(x)Y ′(y), so 5X′Y −4X Y ′ = 0 X′ 4 Y ′=⇒ = = k. X 5 Y Solve ODEs. X′/X = k ⇒X(x) = A ekx, Y ′ 5 5 k = ⇒Y (y) = B e 4 y. Known gaps, conflicts and corrections

  1. No historical archive question page met the unit-match candidate threshold; all subject-level and ambiguous pages remain in datasets/question-archive.json.
  2. 1 video-to-slide associations were not promoted to a match. Candidate rankings are listed in the transcript corpus and video-slide-matches.csv; the slide decks appear separately.
  3. 1 transcript segments have low ASR confidence; they are flagged with timestamps in the transcript blocks and indexed in the corpus.
  4. This is a visibly labelled source-preservation draft. Human page-level semantic and visual review is not complete; no unsupported subject summary or formula correction is claimed.
  5. The 19.26 GiB MP4 files remain hosted on Hugging Face; the full video path/hash and transcript coverage are indexed. The full audio/video was not watched end-to-end.
  6. Formula candidates are machine-extracted text with source locators; use the adjacent original slide page and the formula index before treating a candidate as an authoritative equation.
